(2) coolie fare (charge?) for unloading from the warehouse
and sending to sampans are 215 piastres and 3,696.50
piastres for l00 kilotons respectively; (3) for the portion
for Futsumi coolie fare for warehousing 435 kilotons 1s
391.53 piastres per l00 kilotons; (4) for the portion for
Futsumi coolie fare for unloading 435 kilotons from the ware-
house and rent for sampans are 215 piastres and 935.25
piastres per l00 kilotons respectively; (5) sum of coolie
fare during extra hours, godown charges and other expenses
is 1,234.44 piastres; (6) the total amount for the above
itoms is 7,406.62 piastres; (7) a portion of rice bran has
been taken away by us for use in the oil factory here, and
we are endeavoring by this way to reduce the loss as much
as possible. Please do your best to explain this to the
authorities and obtain their understanding to this means of
covering the loss.

lo Morita Dainan Koshi Hanoi
No.7. Recently the Army has given the following order and
we are expected to cooperate in compliance its spirit. We
hope that things will remain as they are today until we wire
again. Please also wire Mr. Okada and others about it.
"The exploitation enterprises are to be handled for the
time being by the joint cooperation of Nichinan and Dainan
and in order to make efforts for the increase of production,
which is for the promotion of the activities of the enter-
prises, the following steps will be taken: (1) Dainan is to
take charge of all the management of business with Nichinan's
technical assistance; (2) In compliance with the Army's
request, Nichinan wil: send technical experts who will be
appointed by Dainan as non-official staff members and for
whom salaries, etc., will also be paid by Dainan after the
Army's investigation; (3) Dainan will give to Nichinan an
assurance for the payment of all expenditures up to now;
(4) Dainan...the exploitation department... Dainan will
acquire the techniques rapidly and then turn the business
over to independent management efter getting permission
from the Army".
Nichinan
Canton

lo Okura Tientsin
No.42. With regard to the matter of opium, the opinion of
F.I.C. authorities is as follows: (1) The quantity is too
small. No conversations will be undertaken on this matter
1f the minimum amount is less than 10 tons; (2) The price
should be presented from (fixed by?) Tientsin; (3) The
standard may be a little bit below the usual. As regards
the first point, the quantity of return cargo will be very
big if the quantity is 10 tons. In view of the difficulty of
getting ships, we are negotiating to get airplanes to trans-
port it. However, can you ship 10 tons on your side? Please
reply. As regards the second point, we have made an inquiry
of the authorities at Hanoi. The price is 550 yen for
one kilo. There is a big difference as compared with the
price mentioned in your telegram No.29. The price of the
return cargo will be correspondingly high. As for the
third point, with regard to the demands of the F.I.C.
authorities, even if they are at minimum l0.5 and the
moisture content is 20% and the ash content 3.5%, neverthe-
less we are lowering the price so the goods should pass
muster.
Okura
Tokyo
lo Confidential Toakaiun (East Asia Marine Transport) Saigon
Responding to the organization system of the decisive war,
and with a view to making our business active and simple, the
original ll sections in the structure of our compeny are to
be reduoed to seven departments. In Shanghai a general
office

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
offiee for China is to be set up with four departments,
while in Kobe we shall establish a shipping control
office. The division of functions and changes in the
porsonnel are as follow: the person in charge of the
general affairs department (general affairs, personnel
and investigation) and of the requisition purchasing
department of our company is director Mr. Kitagawa.
The person in charge of the transport-communications
department (cargoes and passengers) and concurrently of
the planning denartment and the finance department is spe-
cial deputy Mr. Nakagawa. The person in charge of the
marine business department and the harbor service depart-
ment is special deputy Mr. Kondo. The head of the general
affairs department is Mr. Hikozawa; the head of the trans-
port-communication department is Mr. Mori; the acting head
of the planning department is Mr. Hoshino; the head of the
finance department is Mr. Takeda; the head of the marine
business department is Mr. Irobe; the acting head of the
harbor service department is Vr. Inaka; the (program)
secretary is Mr. Ioku; other secre taries are Messrs.
Otsuka, Sakayori, and Otsuki; the chief of the general
office in China is Mr. Sawai (special deputy); the head of
the general affairs department (general affairs, personnel,
finance, investigation and planning) is Mr. Shimura; the

No.ll0. In order to promote the export of Canton-made raw
silk to French Indochina, the Japanese Embassy is in
negotiation with the French Indochina au thorities on the
question of the repeal of the maximum price at your place.
But when the negotiation was practically concluded, a tele-
gram was received to the effect that the F.I.C. authorities
had not agreed upon the terms on the strength of the contract
which was made between you and us at yen 4, 500 for 70 bags.
Since this is at variance with the facts, we, in the capacity
of a member, do not know what excuse to make to the guild.
We hope you will expound the facts about the exchange adduc-
ing telegrams and invoices and so on for reference of the
French Indochina side and the Mitsui in Hanoi and prove that
the contract was made at CIF yen 9,Oo0 on condition that we
would remit the sum total. Make arrangements for the fore-

Osaka
lc Gosho Saigon
No.62/10. In connection with the agents for exporting cotton
cloth, it has been informally decided that seven firms are
designated to be export agents for yen areas (places where
Japanese currency is in circulation), 19 firms for third
nations while our firm has been designated to be for both.
There are four firms from among the present agents for
F.I.C. which have been disqualified. As we do not know
whether all will be replaced by new firms, we are afraid
that it will be very difficult to obtain the quotas and ship-
ments at present. The qualified firms which undertake the
trade in silk and rayon should have a total amount of more
than 5 millions. But those firms which have an export
record of more than 3 millions for the yen area and 3
millions for third nations may remain independent. Since
Gosho has an amount of 2,400,000 yen for third nations and
5 millions for yen area totalling 7,400,000 yen, therefore it
will remain independent. There are only 2,850,000 yen for
third nations on the part of Horikosi so that it has to be
combined with Yanojoko to establish a new company to be able
to remain as a cooperative central body (?). It is expected
to have 10 independent firms, namely Mitsui, Mitsubishi,
Tomen, Sanko, Gosho, Kawaguchi, Kanematsu, Takashimaya,
Kaei, Mataiohi and io kyodochukakusha (cooperative central
firms?). This will be ascertained by the loth of next month.
Gosho
Saigon

dlt Yamamoto Shigeru 364 Fishigaharacho Takinogawa Tokyo
With rogard to our telegrem dated 15th, whet we want to
explain to you in detail is the fact that we have to
montion to you hastily the absurdities of the dualistic
importation system as seen in the local situation: (1)
the actual import quota is at variance with the ratios
speoified in the apreement between the south and the north.
It will result in a variation of import quantities and in a
general apreading of the commodities whereby antagonistic
competition will ensue between the south and the north;
(2) in view of the shortage of the homeward bound ships in
the north, all olearanoes are made in the south and this
situation, we think will continue for the time being;
(3) in view either of the mutual trade between French
Indoohina and the other places within the Co-Prosperity
Sphere

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
Sphere in the future, or of the policy of localizing the
medicine manufacturing center in the interior, this is
quite contradictory to the main meaning of the national
trade policy of mono-importation structure; (4) in the
home land there has already been a wrong impression that
private firms will make a monopoly of rights in...is
something based on the demand of wartime trade. In view
of the emergence of the Japanese Medicine Exportation
Company in the future, it is convenient to establish the
mono-system at the present time. Please consider the
situation and make an impartial decision.
Kimura Michizo

ECONOMIC CONTRIBITION OF HAINAN ISLAND TO JAPAN
0
2
ω
9
CONFIDENTIAL
November 1943

=== Page 61 === (confidence 0.99)
Summary and Conclusiong
1. The direct economic importance of Hainan Island to
Japan is at present negligible. At the most, during 1943,
Hainan can provide less than one-half of one percent of Japan's
total iron ore requirements, about two percent of the tc tal
selt requirements, a small amount of tin which Japan does not
need, and small amounts of various agricultural products,
especially livestock, meat, and meat products.
2. Japanese and other undonfirmed reports indica ;e
that Hainan may be a potentially rich source of iron ore. It
is not believed, however, that even under optimum circumstances
Japan will be able to depend on Bainan for any considera le pro-
duction of iron ore for several vears.
3. Japan has embarked on a progran for increasing
Hainan's agricultural production, both for the use of troops
stationed on the island an for export to Japan. The island is
a potentially valuable-agricultural recion.
4. In spite of the negligible direct economic importance
of the Islend, its nossession is essential to Japan's program
for exploiting the Southern Regions. It is located squarely
across the shortest shipping route from Janan to northeastern
Indo-China, and overlooks the main shipping route from Japan
south to Thailand, Malaya, Sumatra, Java, and Borneo.
5. In order to facilitate economic exoloitation and to
equip the island as a base, Japan has been developing food
processing facilities, transportation lines, harbors and ports,
airfieldc, and aeaplane, submarine and naval bases.

EXTENT AND IMPORTANCE OF THE JAPAIESE OCGUPATION OF HAINAN
On February 10, 1939, the Jananese occupied the prindipal
coastal towns of Hainan Island without meeting effeetive resis-
tance. By the end of the month they had extended their control
to a strip of coastal plain about 30 miles mide in the northern
part of the jsland and about 2C miles wide around the peri-
phery of the island. According to Chinese reports, Chinese
guerillas still control the mountainous interior. However, the
Japanese would gain no imaediate adventege by trying to crush
the guerillas because they are confined to the interior and can
be prevented from damaging Japanese positions and installations
in the coastal areas. There is some evidence of guerilla
activity, 'but the Japanese apnear to be reasonably satisfied with
the present situation.
The basic importance of the island to the Jananese is de-
rived from its location. It is on the most direct route from
Janan to northeastern Indo-China, and overloolks the main route
south from Janan to Thailand, Malaya, Sumatra, Borneo, and Java.
It is 260 miles from Hongkong, 175 miles from Pal hoi, 225 miles
from Haiphong, and S00 miles from Saigon. The location has
economic significance because of the use that can be made of
the island in protecting transportation lanes of enormous
economic importance.
The island is about 156 mil.s long and about 90 miles
wide with an area of roughly 14,000 square miles. It is
separated from the mainland by a strait about 14 iles wide.
Navigation in the strait is ifficult becauso of sandbars and
reefs, and only srall and medium-sized vessels (up to 10,000
tons) have regularly used it in the rast. The central and
southwestern part of the island is mountainous. The Five-
Fingor Mountain Ranges radiate from the center lito the five
fingers on an outstretched hand, and are bctwoen 5,000 and
6,000 feot high. To tho southeast are the Seven-Finger
Mountains, and to tho southwest is another range which reachos
2.00o to 3,000 foot. Tho northern part is flat mith a fow
scattored hills of volcanic origin. A narrov coastal plain
surrounds most of the island. There are thrac important
rivers, and a number of importan' or potentially important
harbors (seo Appendi: A). The climate is tronical (sie
appondix B). The population, which now nmbors over
2,200,000, is more than 90 percont Chinese, most of them
desoéndants of tho original immigrents from Fukien Province.
The central upland districts of the island are inhabitod by
the primitive Loi and Miao tribes (sco Appondix C).

Yu Lin Ken Bay, 180 11: N, 109° 33' E (H.0 Chart No. 3166).
On the west side of Cape Bastion in the harbor5/ at the head of
Yu Lin Kan bay is the best anchorage on the pouthern coast of

The entrance has a low depth of 4.75 fathoms over a

fathoms deep. The bottom is'soft'mud. The northeast part

tances of from 200 to 500 yards.

others that the Jananese are using this harbor as a sub-
marine base. Wharfs and quay walls have been built as well
as storehouses and other port facilities.

ENDIX C. POPULATION
Hainan Island was sottled about tho second contury
B.Bby Chineso from the coast of Fukion, who established
themsolves in tho coastal regions around Kiungchow and
Hothow. Succeeding wavos of immigration brought tho Loi
tribos, a still primitive group of Thai and Annamito stock,
during the soventh contruy A.D., a fow Mandarin-speaking
Chineso from Pol:ing during tho Soong Dynasty (S60-1272), tho.
Miaos, a primitive group similar to tho Lois, during the
Mihg Dynasty (1058-1564), tho Hakka, the prosont sampan
population, who camo from North China via Fukion a fow
hundred years ago, and a-fow thousand Mohammodans, pro-
bably from Bornco or Singapore.
The first contact vith Europoans probably tool: place
ardund 16s0. Thoro aro Portuguose Catholic graves in the
Kiungchow comotery deting back to 1684 and 1685.
Tho prosont population, which numbered more than
2,200,000 prior to tho Japanesc occupation, is ovor 90 por-
cent Chineso, most of whom live in the coastal rogions.
Tho largost concontration of overseas Chinese, probably the
most progressive clemont on the island, is in the Tanchang
District, whoro out of 490,000 inhabitants about 90,000.
have livod abroad at some. tino.
The Loi number about 200,000. Thoy are dividcd into
two groups, tho "wild" Loi, a primitive, poaceful group in-
habiting tho mountainous intcrior of the island and dopon-
dent on a primitive.form of agriculture, and tho "tame" Loi,
who livo in agricultural communitics nearer tho coast and who
have nover fully submitted to governrent by the Chinosc.
Tho Miaos also inhabit tho inherior. Their modo of
living is much tho same as that of tho "wild" Loi oxcept
that thoy plant more rico.
The Hakka, who number nearly 50,00g comprise the
"sampan" population.
Languago
The prevailing dialect is Hainanoso, which is similar
to the Fuchow and Swatow dielocts of the mainland. Mandarin is
spoken-by a small group of officials and descondents of immi-
grants from north China. The sampan population spoak Hakka,
somo of the Loi spcak a mixturo of Thai and Annanesp, and the
Mino tribes speak a language somowhat similar to Thai.

IRON MINES ON HAINAN ISLAND
Reporte concerning the size, importance, and potenti-
Ahities of iron ore deposits on Hainan Island are confused.
and contradiotory, and conclusions drawn from them have been
more or less controversial. Two deposits have figured largely
in these reports, that at Yu Lin Kan, vhich vas developed
first, and that at Shihluling which has by now far outstripped
the former in importance. A third deposit, at Tengkiu, has
been mentioned, but nothing is known of it other than the fact
that it appears to be located between Lin--Shui and Yu Lin Kan.
No exact estimates can be made of the amounts of ore
now available. to the Japanese. On the basis of all obtainable
evidence, however, it is believed that for the year 1943, the
amount wil1 be somewhere between 50,000 and 100,000 tons of
high grade ore from Shihluling, depending in part on the
amount of shipping available and the successful completion of
harbor developments at Bakli Bay, and possibly as much as
10,000 tons of low grade ore from Yu Lin Kan. The higher
figure represents the estimated maximum output obtainable for
this year. Even so, it amounts to only about one-half of one
percent of Japan's ourrent iron ore requirements on an annual
basis. Given sufficient time and opportunity, however, the
knovn deposits on Hainan could proba'ly be made to contribute
perhans three or four times this amount.
The Shihluling Deposit
In January 1?41, the Japanese announced the discovery
of immense deposits of iron ore (100,000,000 to 400,000,000
tons of 65 percent iron) at Shihluling (Shekluk) in western
Hainan. In April 1941, a dispatch from Hoihow to Osaka
Mainichi reported the decision to build a railroad from the
port of Bekli to the mine. By arch S, 1942, according to
Japanese reports, a double-track railvay vas constructed over
55 kilometers from Bakli to the mine, and according to Domei,
the railways were operating at full capacity by April 1942.
Evidence from Japanese sources ooncerning actual bene-
fits to be derived from the mines includes a telegram stat-
ing that a contract for low-grade iron ore from Japan itself
was cancelled in anticipation of recoiving better ores from
Hainan, and a report that Yasuda l.ogyo is preparing to erect
an iron and steel vorks in Takao (Formosa) vhich vill process
ore from Hainan Island.

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.98)
225
Reports from non-Japanese sources provide a number of
valuable details and tend to indicate a considerably more con-
servative picture of the immediate and potential importance of
themine.
The location vas believed to be about 1090 10' E, 190
-17: N, but a recent report places it at 1090 10' E, 190 10'
N., or south instead of north of the Song Hoi River. The
exact location, of course, cannot be proved under present con-
ditions:
Another report on the size of the deposit, a cable
from New Delhi dated July 31, 1943, states that it is 600
meters long, 200 meters wide and 50 meters deep, and that it
contains.between 20,000,000 and 25,000,000 tons of ore con-
taining 60 to 66 percent iron.
Relatively recent reports have stated that 10,000
laborers were engaged in mining the ore from the hiliside by
hand, pending the arrival of machinery. They are said to have
been imported from Hon kong on a ono year's contract, and to
be housed in long wooden godons. rorking and hoalth conditions
are believed to be bad. Although rice supplies from Indo-
China are anparently adequate, medical supplios are not, and
malaria and black vater fever are reported provalent. Opium
is distributed to conteract the vorkers' dissatisfaction vith
conditions. Nages, part of vhich aro paid in special numbered

var arrived at Bakli at tho end of 1942.
No absoluto confirmation of tho existence of the rail-
road from the mine to the port has yet been received. The
fact that it has been complcted and is now in oporation is
impliod both diroctly and indirectly in numerous unconfirmed
reports, including one from Japanese sources vhich states
that the combined output of ore from the Shihluling and Yu Lin
Kan fields during the period Septomber 1940 to September 1942
vas about 35,000 tons. It is believed, hovever, that the rail-
road vas almost certainly not operating at capacity as carly
as April 1942.
The mine is in the most inaccessible part of the is-
land. The railroad  ould have to be built through thick
tropical jungle, over very rough country vith tropical rains
for six months of the year. In the opinion of engineers fami-

The Yu Lin Kan Deposit
In May 1940, the Japanese announced the discovery of a
largo iron deposit (6,000,000 tons of about 40,porcent iron)
at Tin Tuk near Yu Lin Kan, and their in ention to develop it.
A British report of May 1941 stated that production at Yu Lin
Kan was only six tons of ore daily. Production may have in-
creased since then, but the mine has not figured in reports
sinco tho opening up of the far larger and richer deposit at
Shihluling, and the Japanese are ovidently considerably less
intorested in it than they wore. Transportetion vould not be
a scrious problem from this mine because it is only about six
miles from Yu Lin Kan Harbor.
Other Deposits
Thore are unconfirmed reports of other iron donosits
on tho island, but no indication that the Japanese are trying
to dovelop any of tham oxcept poasibly one at Tengkiu (T'eng-
chiao) about vhich nothing is known.

NOTE ON SOUROES
Except for three or four relatively recent geologibal
and geographical survey raports, there is no reliable information
on Hainan Island. The most reliable reports are:
1. Reports of Lingnan University expeditfons of 1929-
1936, under Professors Hoffman and McClure, which give a
considerable amount of reliable information on the island.
Unfortunately they covered only a small fraction of the island
and were concerned primarily with climate, flora and fauna, eto.
2. Reports of the Chinese Geological Survey of Kwangtung
and Kwangsi Provinces, which has done some accurate geological
work in the northern part of the island.
3. Reports and notes of S. E. Lavrov, who was a party of
Chinese engineers conducted a geological reconnaissance over
the northwest part of Hainan between January and Septenber,
1936, This survey was undertaken on behalf of Marshall Chang
Chi Tong: Goverror General of Kwangtung Province, and Mr. Robert
de Vos, a Belgian financier.
There are very few books on Hainan, either in English or
Chinese, Most of these were written 30 or 40 years ego and deal
with the island in a general way, repeat hearsay evidence,
and are quite inaccurate.
Recent secondary reports or Hainan have been very contradictory.
Estimates of mineral resoucces range all the way from virtually
none to deposits of 20,000,000 tons of copper ore and 400,000,000
tons of iron ore. It is believed that reports describing Hainan
as extremely rich in mineral resources were based on Chinese
hsien reports, which as a rule are generally inaccurato
concerning mineral wealth, and Japanese propaganda statements
such as radio broadcasts and articles in Japanese-controlled
newspapers such as the Peking Chronicle and the Shanghai Times.
The most reliable recent secondary report is believed to be that
of the United States Geological Survey, which reports that Hainan
is extremely poor in mineral resources.
The attached report is based primarily on the three
original survey reports listed above, especially the reports
and notes of 3. E. Lavrov, togothor with official statistics and
trade returns.

Trade Management Association, Saigon
Name: Sino-Japanese Trade Federation Organization. Under the
president there are the standing directors. The general affairs
bureau consists of the president's office, the planning depart-
ment, the first business department (agricultural products and
foodstuffs), the second business department (fabrics and grocery
articles), the third business department (chemical medicines
and reagents) while the fourth business department (in accord-
ance with the local relations) is to be divided into the account-
ing section and the administrative and supervisory section. All
department heads hold posts as standing directors. Department
head Mr. Saito is concurrently the head of the administrative
and supervisory section of the fourth business department. All
other staff members hold single and special posts. If the holding
of concurrent posts will hinder the accelerating of business
operations of the Trade Management Association, then those
business firms which prove to have had experience in the past
will supply its members. What you should pay attention to is
that the direct object of the Federation is to practice monistio
control of the import and export trade. Therefore those who are
not members who have received the extrustment of the Federation
are not allowed to deal in the import and export trade. Details
are given in letter.

Uneikai (Transportation Management Association) Saigon
At a time when the war situation has become extremely signi-
ficant and when the power of transportation has become a key
to determine the fate of the battle, the epochal development
of the loading and unloading function of the harbors has been
positively thought of in order to enhance the efficiency of
transportation to the maximum degree. Recently veteran
inspectors have be en injecting new blood in overcoming all
kinds of difficulties which had been obstacles until then.
Judging by the present conditions in your place, are there
any unsatisfactory points which have been obstacles ? Please
gather up and wire in haste all constructive opinions which
would accelerate the efficiency of loading and unloading to
some degree if these obstaces are overcome. We are to start
unyielding negotiations with the different Ministries of the
Government by basing on reports from the different places.
We shall also try all means for its realization in all the
local places.
Koomubutyoo (The Harbor Master of the
Harbor Office)
Tokyo

Nagoya
dlt Futsuin (French Indochina Co.) Saigon
The commodity tariff regulations have been modified. Beginning
with February 16, commodity tariff is also imposed on export
goods. For medical earthenwares, and table wares, if the manu-
facturing cost for one set is under 50 sen, no tariff is to be
imposed; for those above 50 sen the tariff is 60%. For table-
wares whose selling price is above 50 yen, the tariff is 80%;
for electric utensils, 40%. If the calculation of the imposi-
tion of the new tariff is to be paid, then the usual 30% has
been increased to 60%. The prices of your contracted goods
have been increased by that much. Although we want to continue
the present contracts with the French Indochina companies,still

1c Igetasan, Saigon
No.367. I am referring to your wire concerning the fixed
quantity packing of rice addressed to the head of the
Transportation Department. The weighing at the same time
of two bags of 50 kilogram packing rice, and the practising
of 2% increase or decrease of the fixed quantity before load-
ing on the rails, are against the basic meaning of the
principle of the fixed quantity packing, and there would be
the usual and unrecoverable chances for shortage as there
were in the past. Although there are more or less difficulties
right at the beginning, but once the system of the fixed quan-
tity packing is established and seriously enforced from the
beginning, the French Indochina side will be made to study
and improve their job of packing rice. As a result the
delivery and loading will be allowed to go on smoothly. I
am thinking that in this way the need for helping hands can
also be reduced. Therefore please put the above into practice
immediately.
Head of Cereals Transportation Dep't. of Mitsui

raffic on March 6, 1944
Saigon
1c Accounting Department Head, Mitsui, Tokyo
No.639. Referring to wire No.406 from the head of correspondence
department of the central company dated February 29, please
reply in haste what shall we do with the different enterprises
undertaken by our firm in connection with the accounting readjust-
ment of the new Mitsui Bussan Company. The following is what is
regarded by us as desirable: (1) All the different enterprises
should be taken formally as somet hing outside of the Company and
the purchases and sales of raw materials and manufactured goods
should be regarded as a kind of buying and selling outside of
the Company. (2) Accounts for con tingent receipts and disburse-
ments and for those accounts for the different enterprises which
have not been settled should be opened in the statement of assets
and liabilities while only the monthly balances are to be record-
ed. (3) If monthly closing of accounts is necessary for the
different enterprises, then at the end of each month statements
of assets and liabilities, profit and loss accounts, detailed
inventories(?) and other necessary documents and statements
should be made separately together with those for the others
such as the mining, the forestry exploitation, the wooden boats,
etc. (4) Running expenses for the different enterprises should
be treated as hitherto, but as soon as they are recorded in the
new account-books, the different portions (of the running expenses)
should be entered into the accounts of the respective different
enterprises to be counter-cancelled with the profit and loss
accounts of the respective enterprises. (5) As the dealing in
the raw materials needed in accordance with the above desire of
ours is increased the actual accomplishments of the enterprises
of our shops would se inaccurate if only the usual selling of
the manufactured goods is to be recorded. (6) The capital funds
allotted to the different enterprises, the balances of the above
after being used up, the commodities on stock, items of proper-
ties, contingent receipts and di sbursements, and keishi(?) will
be treated as pending.
Head of the Branch
(Origin not stated)

Yours received. The export agreement for the current year has
not been reached. as there is not any prospect of getting the
export permit, please stop attempts to make arrangement for
obtaining shipping space for a while. Even if the agreement has
been reached, as priority will be given to the exportation to
Japan, no quota will be allotted to this portion if the quantity
agreed upon is something like that of the past year. Therefore
we are depending on the pool. I think it is better to exert our-
selvos to obtain the quota as a kind of contract with the pool.
As the demand of the special authorities was supplied by gather-
ing up these commodities originally destined for the contract,
it is necessary to buy new commodities to fill up the contract
still remaining unattended to. Please approve a price increase
of 7oo yen for this shipment to make it the same with those which
have not been shipped out yet. I think anythin  under this price
will also not be possible for the pool to undertake. The price
has already been estimated at 75o yen when the new commodities
have still not appeared. There is little hope for the price to
get low.

Traffic on March 29, 1944:
Saigon
Press Asahi, Tokyo
Article on assigned topic. Due to the fact that the French
Indochina Administration, in connection with its national
policy toward the Annamites, has been adopting an antagonistic
attitude toward the external emergency of any kind of movement
under the name of "independence", the residential Indians,
irrespective of the enc curaging news that the Indian National
Army, with the cooperation of the Imperial Army,has succeeded
in thrusting into the Indian region, are nevertheless taking a
rather cool and unconcerned attitude. Those Indians who are
now residing in French Indochina are only concerned to do their
best not to offend the authorities of the dministration. While
most of these are coming from that part of India under ex-French
regime, such as Pondicherry, Karikal, etc.,they are spreading
persistently the selfish sentiment of making money rather than
taking interest in the independence movement. Thro ugh the
beforehand exertion of the authorities concerned, only formal
thanks were expressed to the governing body under Bose by ways
of statements and broadcasts. Nevertheless there was not even
one flag of the Provisional Government hanging out on the
streets in Saigon and holon. Toward the present encouraging
development, nothing at all in the fom of concrete steps has
been shown. End. (This article has passed through the censor
of the Information Department of the local Army).
Fukazawa
Saigon

Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch

NO. 2440, April 17, 1944,
American Embassy, Chungking.
NFIDENTIAL
RECENT REPORTS ON JAPANESE ECONOMIC ACTIVITIES
APPEARING IN GERMAN NEWSPAPERS
(Supplied to the Enbassy by the Information Department
of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, Chungking.)
1. Production of Minerals in Inner Mongolia. Accord-
ing to a report from Kalgan, the production of pig iron
in "Mengchiang" (Inner Mongolia) has progressed so satis-
factorily in recent months that supplies will soon be
available for export. The output of iron ore from the
Lungyen mines, which was retarded during the summer
months owing to labor shortage, has again increased con-
siderably. The output of other riches of the soil in
"Mengchiang" has likewise made considerable progress.
In the first place tnere has been an increase in the
production of asbestos and graphite, the greater part
of which is being delivered to Japan. (National Zeitung,
December 26, 1943.)
2. Aviation Lubricant from Coconuts. In Japan
lubricating oil for airplane engines has lately been
manufactured from coconuts. Thirtyone inaustrial enter-
prises are engaged in this production, in which a secret
process of evaporation and pressure is applied. Experts
classify the oil as being a high grade lubricant. (Stutt-
garter N.S. Kurier, December 7, 1943.)
3. Production of Cotton in Occupied China. In an
article dealing with cotton as a strategic raw material,
it is written that the Japanese intend to increase the
cultivation of cotton in national (central?) and north
China by 1946 to such an extent that the total yield will
reach 10,000,000 piculs (1,000,000 piculs equa1 c0,000
tons). In order to realize tnis proram, it will, however,
be necessary to get a strong grip on Hupeh Province which
so far is still in the fignting zone. (Rheinische West-
falische Zeitung, November 30, 1943.)
4. Agricultural Development of Manchuria. The Japa-
nese Cabinet decided to support "Manchukuo" in its effort
to cultivate the sungari district and l3 other areas by
ordering 150 experts to "Manchukuo" and by supplying all
the necessary machinery. (Donauzeitune, Rovember 26,
1943.)
5. Philippine Sugar and Alconol Production. The
Philippine sugar production from 1939 to 1943 was as
fol1ows (in metric tons): 1939/40 - 1,025,329; 1940/41 -
1,044,808; 1941/42 - 1,060,000 and 1942/43 - 600,000.
According to a statement by the Minister of Agriculture
and Commerce of the Philippines, the Government expects
that within one to two years the country will produce
sufficient sugar to cover its entire fuel requirements
from the alcohol manufactured thereof. Owing to the war
sugar production fell off considerably in 1942, which
resulted in a tremendous rise in price, namely, from 10
pesos per bag in normal times to 2o0 pesos at present.
(Deutscher Bericht F.0. Licht, November 11, 1943.)
6. Reorganization

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
6. Reorganization of Foreign Trade Companies in
pan. The establishment of a Japanese Ministry of Muni-
cions has already led to far-reaching recrganization mea-
sures. Whereas the execution of new economic plans pre-
viously often stranded owing to differences of opinion,
now the will of only one authority is decisive.  The first
reorganization measure effected concerned Japanese foreign
trade. Although a monopoly enterprise already existed
in this domain, known as the Koeki Eidan, it was dependent
upon three different ministries and this effectively
checked its activities. Now it has been placed under
the Ministry for Greater East Asiatic Affairs which on
November 3 decreed a complete reorganization of Japanese
foreign trade, excluding the great number of those typical
Japanese small export firms from this kind of trade.
Whereas hitherto 6, O0o firms have been engaged in
Japanese foreign trade, arter the completion of the reor-
¿anization only about 600 will remain. A total of 5,400
enterprises will have to choose some cther activity, and
they will be granted subsidies up to l00,Ooo yen each
from a special fund provided for this purpose. The firms
continuing in the former line of business will all have
to work under the noeki widan which will assign them their
activities. The average minimum turnover of the firms
which will be allowed (to continue operations) must have
reached at least Yen 5,000,000 in 1941 and 1942 derived
from trade in at least five difierent roups of goods.
It is possible that an amalgamation of several riras will
be arranged. Firms exportine furs, brushes, hats, fiber-
refuse, pottery and classware are oxeapt from the new
regulations. They will, hewever, be red o that tnere
will be one corporation for cach : rtncu. ine saall import
firms will not be amaleumatea und they are consequently
doomed to disappear. they wilt, towever, be paid indem-
nities from a special funa created by the Japanese Trade
Association (nandelsverein). ror the present tnis new
regulation of Japanese foreien trade does not apply to
trading with the occupied southern zone, which, until
further notice, will be selely uider the military adminis-
tration which may, however, charge indiviual firms with
the execution of certain tasks.(breslauer neueste .ach-
richten, December 2, 1943.)
7. Industrial Development in Rorea. The light metals
industry of Korea is colng to be considerably developed
in the near future. Electric power stations, both nyaro-
electric and others, will produce the necessary power for
the factories. Lig dams have been built in Suiho (south
Korea), in nokai, Anosenko, Funei, hanko and Seutigsno
(spelling?). The cheap electric power has enabled a
number of horean towns to become important industrial
centers. The Korean industry imports considerable amounts
of raw materials from the occupied areas in the south.
For a long time past bauxite from southeast Asia tas been
smelted in Korea, while oil and soap factories make use
of sesame seed, flax and cotton seed, and soya beans from
"Manchukuo" and Korea, and lately also a large quantity
of copra from southeast Asia.
The reorganization

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
The reorganization of Japanese industry has necessi-
tated great changes in the Korean textile industry. The
factories have either been stopped or converted, while
the heavy industry, viz., the metallurgical, machinery
and machine tool plants, is increasing. Packing paper
is manufactured in the Oji paper mills, which get the
timber from the upper part of the Yalu River district.
The Hokusen paper mills in Kissho now produce fiber
for manufacturing artificial silk. The itchitsu trust
produces ammonium sulfate in two of its factories. The
great expansion of the Korean leather industry is also
worth mentioning as it benefits from the abunaance of
high grade and cheap labor. (Rheinisch-Westfalische
Zeitung, December 3, 1943.)
8. Reorganization of Japanese Textile Industry.
The third reorganization of the Japanese textile Industry
for war purposes was completed during the first part of
1943. Only 35 percent of the textile enterprises (cotton?)
retained their independence, 18 percent were sold or leased,
3 percent were converted to otner inaustrial purposes,
7 percent were kept as reserve factories, 1 percent will
be used for storing machinery, 10 percent of the factories
will be used for unspecified purposes, 8 percent will
be kept running in cooperation with other enterprises,
7 percent were rebuilt and nodernized, while 1 percent
was amalgamated with factories naving sinilar production.
(Note: Totals 98 percent.) Only 10 out of 54 cotton
spinning mills remained after the reorganization, their
total capital anounting to Yen 588,O00,O00. The number
will probably be still further reduced when production
has peen concentrated still more as recently announced.
The reorganization of the artificlal silk industry
was started at the end of l940 and was conpleted, at least
to a certain extent, t is summmer (1943). As only 13
out of 33 companies were left, witn 17 out of 75 factories,
this proves that a very far reachin concentration of
production nas been carried out.
In the wool industry the concentration of the spinning
mills was completed in the sprinc of 1943, Whereas the
reorganization of the weaving industry was only started
in March l943. The average number of weaving looms is
going to be raised to S00 per factory. There is a general
tendency for favoring the enterprises which have both
weaving and spinning mills. (Koelnischne Zeitung, Novem-
ber 24, 1943.)

June 14, 1944
JAPANESE INDUSTRY
In August, 1937, the area around Tientsin was occupied by the Japanese.
From August, 1937, until December, 1941, the economic life in this area was more
or less normal, but controlled to a great extent by the Japanese. During this
time the Japanese still permitted British shipping. The Americans, however, were
the least affected. In spite of the Japanese control, the Chinese still collected
the taxes and were allowed to keep them.
From an economic standpoint, the largest and most important revenues
were those ccllected in connection with the Chinese Maritime Customs. Japan did
not openly take over the Customs until after December 7, 194l. Prior to December,
l94l, the British and American Maritime Commissioners cooperated to some extent
with the Japanese in order to adjust themselves to the Japanese occupation of
Tientsin. The details of this phase of revenue matters are not known but it is
believed that after 1937 the Japanese exerted some influence and control over
the customs.
The Japanese, after 1937, controlled the leading transportation arteries 
and trade centers. In running the railroads and factories in that area, the
Japanese would use the Chinese that would cooperate with them, selecting from
that group those who would best serve Japanese interests. The Japanese control
extended to the requirement that passes were necessary to enter or leave the
Tientsin area. Just a few months prior to the outbreak of war in 194l it was
2
necessary to pay a toll to enter the British concession in Tientsin.
CS
The prices of all commodities rose during the period of the Japanese
occupation and the cost of imported goods increased enormously. The Japanese
printed and circulated money which within a short time after its issuance was
worth less than the Chinese National Note, the latter being somewhat stabilized
by an arrangement with the Federal Reserve System. The incorporation of this
Japanese money into the Chinese economy will be a burden to China after the defeat
of Japan. Because of currency problems, cigarettes became the medium of exchange.
Japan maintained political control through pro-Japanese Chinese. Most
of these Chinese were opportunists. Japan had advisers attached to the principal
offices. There were no elections, but it should be noted that elections in
this part of China were quite unusual occurrences even before the Japanese
occupation.
There was very little change in the civilian life after the Japanese
occupation in 1937. Prices went higher, but after December, 1941, prices
skyrocketed as the supply of commodities decreased. Imports were almost
unobtainable. The Japanese exercised control over civilian life by instituting
a system of innoculations and vaccinations. A curfew was put into effect, but
the stringency of its enforcement varied from time to time. It was required that
everyone in the area be identified.

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
Schools ware closed at the outset of the occupation, but were opened
bally as the Japanese arranged for the students to be taught according to
panese principles.
The Japanese made no extensive arrangements to abolish unemployment,
but did oemmence various road-building projects in this area. Work was also
dene toward the construction of railroads into the interior. In February, 194l,
the Japanese were double-tracking railroads in Korea. The Japanese paid the
Chinese laborers with Japanese money.
In the Tientsin area the Japanese did not employ many repressive
measures as they found very little resistance there. Strict repressive
measures were employed only in the sectors where the Chinese showed resistance.
The plight of the Chinese farmer was pitiable. He would work for the Japanese
all day constructing roads and at night Chinese guerillas would force him to
tear them up.
The Chinese engaged in widespread smuggling activities which the
Japanese tried to control. Most of the smuggling was into the British concession.
As a measure of control the Japanese constructed an electrified wire fence
around the British concession. This was done prior to December, 194l. After
December 7, 19ll, all Allied subjects were interned in the British concession.
In the Tientsin area the only resources available in marketable
quantities were cotton, wheat, tobacco, limited amounts of rice, inferior
deposits of coal, and limited quantities of iron ore. Efforts were being made
to increase the cotton acreage to replace the decrease in imports from the
United States. There were no factories in this area, but there were cotton
mills in Tsinan, Tsingtao, and Tientsin.
Prior to the Japanese occupation, Chinese labor maintained a form of
guild system, but this faded away under the Japanese rule.
With reference to matters of finance prior to December 7, 194l, the
Japanese did not openly intervene in the banking activity of American and English
banks. After that date, however, Japanese managers were placed in these banks.
The same procedure was true as to American and British owned corporations. After
December 7, 194l, the Japanese maintained a fixed rate of exchange with the Swiss,
but the Japanese as well as all other nationals, were anxious to obtain American
currenoy.
The transportation facilities which had been run by the British and
Americans were taken over by the Japs after December 7, 194l. A Scotish railroad
man is reported to have said that when the Japanese took over changes were made
in the railroad facilities in two months which he had been unsuccessfully trying
to put into effect for sixteen years. This statement was made with reference
to the Peking-Mukden Railroad. The Japs were constructing new railraods in
northern China, but no details regarding this were available. The Japanese took
over the Chinese airlines and were putting in additional lines. An airline
between Tientsin and Jeho in Southern Manchuria was inaugurated.
Desirable bomb objectives in this area would be the bridges across the
Yellow River, the railroads and the cotton mills in the above-mentioned towns.
- 2 -

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
The Japanese occupation had very little effect on Chinese morale.
After 5000 years of grief and turmoil, three or four years of the Japanese
occupation did not have a very appreciable effect.
Concerning Japan proper it was the impression of most of the British
and Americans living there that the Japanese were experiencing shortages in
many essential commodities, but later events proved that actually the Japanese
were building up reserves in order to prosecute the war. The Japanese did
not appear to have any original processes and were relying entirely on
methods which they had copied from the British, Americans or Germans.
Although the Japanese were apparently building up reserves prior to the war,
there was no indication that they had any surpluses. The Japanese people,
as a whole, were complaining about the synthetic and imitation articles
2
which it had to use. It was noted that the Japanese were importing about
50% of the rice consumed. This was the most important commodity and was
Imported from French Indo China. In this connection, the importance of the
Japanese merchant marine is obvious. It was noted that in Tokio during August
of 1938 or 1939 there was a severe water shortage. The Tokio water supply
was only in operation for two hours in the morning and two hours in the evening.
No specific information concerning the location of manufacturing
plants in Tokio could be obtained although it was ascertained that a new
factory was built near Yokahama in 1938. It is located five miles to the
west of the city of Yokahama on the north side of the road to Kamakura.
Regarding labor conditions, it was noted that in the first six
months of 1937, just before Japan became engaged in war in China, there were
more strikes than there had ever been throughout the history of Japan. All
of the labor troubles disappeared, however, after the commencement of
hostilities in China.
Insurance men considered Japan very inflammable, particularly in
the typhoon season, that is, in early fall. Although American and British
insurance companies issued insurance on various plants in Tokio, the com-
panies' representatives were not allowed to inspect the plants. In the early
part of l9ll, it was noticed that the Japanese government was not permitting
its domestic insurance companies to renew insurance policies on properties
abroad. The effect of this was to limit Japanese liabilities abroad after the
war started.
Concerning transportation facilities, it was learned from an in-
dividual who was a resident in Tokio until 19ll that there was no perceptible
increase in domestic air routes. It was also learned that there is but one
railroad line with double track which runs along the coast from Tokio to
Kobe. The destruction of this line would severely hamper Japanese transportatio
facilities. Between Atami and Shimizu, two points on the railroad between
Tokio and Yokahama,there are probably as many as thirty railroad tunnels.
As possible bombing objectives for the Allies there are the following:
the ship yards to the west of Kobe on the Harima Bay, the Kobe Steel Works
located on the south side of the railroad line from Kobe to Osaka, and the
oil storage facilities located on a point jutting out into Tokio Bay, midway
between Tokio and Yokahama.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DIXISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 18, 1944
ECA:
.The attached interesting study, entitled "The Postr
Readjustment of Japan's Economy", arrives at certain cor
clusions, stemming from three major assumptions which may
be summarized as follows:
(1) There will be a period of four or five years fol
lowing the ermistice and before the final peace settlement
is reached, during which period "sone international organiza-
tion will be given absolute control of Japan's internal
0
4
(2) In order to assure peace in the Pacific we must
have a "viable, contented and peace-abiding Japan", and
to that end Japan must be rendered "economically satisfied".
(3) The United States, as well as the other United
Nations, rests under some sort of moral obligation to re-
habilitate Japan. "The rehabilitation of Japan is a job
which must be done and done thoroughly." The study ahounds o
with such phrases as "must be found", "must be made", "steps 
should be taken", "arrengements will have to be made", and
"efforts should be made".
If one accepts these three assumptions as sound, the
C
study offers an interesting analysis of the problems which
would have to be solved by an international organization in
readjusting Japan's economy in the post-war period. However,
the fact should be taken into consideration that the American
people, in their present temper, probably would not agree
with the assumptions.
In regard to the first assumption, namely, that "some
international organization will be given absolute control
of Japan's internal organization" for some four or five years
after the armistice, it is difficult to see how any inter-
national organization could long maintain effective control
over Japan's "internal organization" without the backing of
a powerful occupying force. (It is stated in the study that
a military occup tion of Japen of only two to six months is
visualized.) The Japanese are a patriotic, shrewd and ag-
力
gressive people, who will greatly resent foreign control of
RM/R
their internal affairs and will do everything in their
25
power to return such control to their own hands at the
earliest possible moment. It is noted that the study con-
templates the "absolute cooperation" of the Japanese with
2
StateDegpt
the internationa
Japanese Affairs Division
friim Dickover

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
the international supervisory organization, but on page 12
it is suggested that "the echievement of this degree of co-
operation may call for some harsh and stringent measures".
Exactly how the "harsh and stringent measures" are to be
applied without the use of force, after the withdrawal of
the occupying armies, is not explained. The consensus of
opinion among those who have given thought to the subject
appears to be that the actual military occupation of Japan
should be as brief as is compatible with the accomplishment
of two objectives, namely, the complete destruction of
Japan's military machine and the bringing home to the Japa-
nese people of a realization of the full extent of their
defeat. After that, it is believed that, given reasonable
economic opportunity and such advice as may be desired, the
Japanese people should be left alone to work out their own
salvation in their own way and their own time.
In regard to the second assumption, namely, that a
viable, contented and economically-satisfied Japen must be
created if we are to have peace in the Pacific, it would
seem that general opinion in the United States does not
agree with this theory. It will reasonably be asserted that
peace in the Pacific in the future will be maintained by
our overwhelming superiority in arms, and not by creating a
contented Japan. The question will arise of whether or not
it is possible to keep Japan "economically satisfied" and
still militerily weak, and it will be asserted that we can
allow Japen only as great a degree of economic prosperity
as will be compatible with continued military weakness. It
will also be claimed that persuading Japan to be good by
rendering her "economically satisfied" will be too much
like bribing a child with candy to induce it to refrain
from burning down the house.
Mr. Grew said in his speech of December 29, 1943:
"Just retribution must and shall be meted out to the enemy
so that the people of those countries shall be forever
cured of the illusion that aggression pays. Their false
philosophy can never be discredited until the results are
brought home to them in defeat, humiliation, and bitter
loss." It is believed that this expresses the opinion of
most Americans who have given thought to the matter. Would
it be consistent with this general theory to proceed im-
mediately after the armistice to create a "contented" and
"economically-satisfied" Japan?
There is also a question if the American people will
desire a "viable" Japan ("viable" in this connection 1s
taken to mean "capable of growth and development"). It is
believed that the majority of Americans will be of the
opinion that Japan has been much too "viable" already.
It 1s

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
It is also believed that the third assumption, namely,
that the United Nations are under some sort of moral obliga-
tion to rehabilitate Japan and to reform her economic
structure, will not be found to be acceptable to the American
people. It will be asked, "Why should the United Nations try
to rescue Japan from the consequences of her folly?" It will
undoubtedly be argued that Japan must rehabilitate herself,
as the United Nations will have their hands full rehabilitating
themselves. The question will also be asked why we should
rehabilitate Japan, an enemy country, instead of rehabilita-
ting our friends and Allies. Are we not under more of an
obligation to try to rehabilitate such countries as China,
Greece and Poland? It is obvious that we cannot restore
the domestic economics of all of the countries of the world,
and it 1s believed that American sentiment would favor
helping our friends rather than our enemies.
The above comments are based on JA's estimate of Ameri-
can opinion today; it may well be that that opinion will
change with the progress of the war.
It would be useful to have alternative studies compiled,
assuming that American public opinion will compel the post-
war treatment of Jepan to be based on one or more of the
following considerations:
(1) There will be no internationel control of Japan's
internal economy following a brief military occupation.
(2) Peace in the Pacific will be maintained in the
future by our superior armed forces and not by creating a
contented,economically-satisfied Japen.
(3) The United Nations rest under no obligation to re-
habilitate Janan.
(4) The Japanese must work out their own salvation, in
their own peculiar way and under their own peculiar laws,
customs, and traditions, with such advice from us as may be
desired.
In each case, what economic policies should be adopted,
and what measures taken vis-à-vis Japan by the United States,
in order best to serve our own interests?
ER.S.
JA;ERD:MHP
